I’m not totally sure I’d call it worried, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’m thinking about things, like stuff I need to do or conversations from the day or whatever, but it doesn’t always feel like fear or panic. It’s more like my brain just won’t settle down, if that makes sense.

Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to explain, because it’s not like I made some big decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… stopped.

I used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It was something I looked forward to, even if I was tired after work or didn’t feel like leaving the condo at first. Once I got there, the music and moving around and just being around people usually helped. But I haven’t been going. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.

And painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.

I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.

I don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.

It’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.

It’s more like I just… stopped.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It was something I looked forward to, even if I was tired after work or didn’t feel like leaving the condo at first. Once I got there, the music and moving around and just being around people usually helped. But I haven’t been going. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.\n\nAnd painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 26 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"The really obvious cycling between that charged-up stretch and this crash has been going on for about a month, since the mortgage became real.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.

Buying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. And since then—probably about a month now—I’ve noticed this really obvious back-and-forth, where I had that more charged-up stretch and then this crash after it. The crash part especially has been hard to ignore.

So I guess the stressful thing was the mortgage becoming real. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.

But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.\n\nBuying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. And since then—probably about a month now—I’ve noticed this really obvious back-and-forth, where I had that more charged-up stretch and then this crash after it. The crash part especially has been hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the stressful thing was the mortgage becoming real. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that more charged-up stretch, did you feel unusually energized or “up” compared with your normal self?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 26 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Earlier in the month I felt almost lit up and unstoppable, then I’d snap at my mom or my supervisor over little questions that normally wouldn’t bother me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I did. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.

And at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.

So yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.

But during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. I remember almost being surprised by it, because I’d look at the time and realize how little I’d slept, but I didn’t feel tired in the way I should have.

It wasn’t like I was resting well, exactly. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.

It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.\n\nAnd at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.\n\nBut during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that more charged-up stretch, were you doing more activities or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. That was actually one of the things that made me realize something was off, because it wasn’t just that I had more energy in my head — I was doing a lot more.

I’d get home from work, and normally after staring at numbers all day I want to eat something, maybe watch TV, maybe do one chore if I’m being responsible. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.

And then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. Like I’d be writing down what I wanted to change, what it might cost, what order to do things in, looking up supplies, comparing ideas. Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates again, even though the mortgage was already done, like I needed to keep monitoring everything.

And on top of that I started setting up ideas for side jobs, like ways I could bring in extra money. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.

That’s actually been one of the harder things to explain, because it’s not like I made some big decision to stop doing things. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.\n\nAnd painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.\n\nAnd painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.\n\nBuying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. And since then—probably about a month now—I’ve noticed this really obvious back-and-forth, where I had that more charged-up stretch and then this crash after it. The crash part especially has been hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the stressful thing was the mortgage becoming real. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you feel unusually energized or “up” compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.\n\nAnd at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.\n\nBut during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. I remember almost being surprised by it, because I’d look at the time and realize how little I’d slept, but I didn’t feel tired in the way I should have.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was resting well, exactly. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, were you doing more activities or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That was actually one of the things that made me realize something was off, because it wasn’t just that I had more energy in my head — I was doing a lot more.\n\nI’d get home from work, and normally after staring at numbers all day I want to eat something, maybe watch TV, maybe do one chore if I’m being responsible. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.\n\nAnd then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. Like I’d be writing down what I wanted to change, what it might cost, what order to do things in, looking up supplies, comparing ideas. Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates again, even though the mortgage was already done, like I needed to keep monitoring everything.\n\nAnd on top of that I started setting up ideas for side jobs, like ways I could bring in extra money. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that more charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to count it exactly, because it wasn’t like one clean block where I can say, “It started Monday and ended Friday.” The really obvious back-and-forth — like being all charged up and then crashing — has been going on for about a month now, basically since the mortgage became real and it wasn’t just an idea anymore.\n\nThe charged-up parts would come in stretches within that, but the whole pattern has been about a month. Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it at first as “talking more,” exactly, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining things that were important. But my sister definitely noticed.

Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.\n\nBut during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. I remember almost being surprised by it, because I’d look at the time and realize how little I’d slept, but I didn’t feel tired in the way I should have.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was resting well, exactly. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, were you doing more activities or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That was actually one of the things that made me realize something was off, because it wasn’t just that I had more energy in my head — I was doing a lot more.\n\nI’d get home from work, and normally after staring at numbers all day I want to eat something, maybe watch TV, maybe do one chore if I’m being responsible. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.\n\nAnd then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. Like I’d be writing down what I wanted to change, what it might cost, what order to do things in, looking up supplies, comparing ideas. Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates again, even though the mortgage was already done, like I needed to keep monitoring everything.\n\nAnd on top of that I started setting up ideas for side jobs, like ways I could bring in extra money. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that more charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to count it exactly, because it wasn’t like one clean block where I can say, “It started Monday and ended Friday.” The really obvious back-and-forth — like being all charged up and then crashing — has been going on for about a month now, basically since the mortgage became real and it wasn’t just an idea anymore.\n\nThe charged-up parts would come in stretches within that, but the whole pattern has been about a month. That’s when I started noticing, or maybe when it became harder to ignore, because everything felt more intense around then.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it at first as “talking more,” exactly, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining things that were important. But my sister definitely noticed.\n\nI remember one conversation where I was going on about the condo stuff, and it was like I jumped from equity to taxes to paint colors and then back to what I should do first, and she finally kind of laughed but also looked overwhelmed and said I was talking really fast. Like she couldn’t even get a word in.\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, once she pointed it out. It was like there was this pressure behind it, like if I didn’t keep going I’d lose the thread or the idea would disappear. I wasn’t trying to cut her off, but I kept pushing forward, almost like the words were coming faster than I could sort them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. At the time it didn’t feel like “I’m being unrealistic” or anything. It felt like I could just handle everything if I pushed hard enough.

I honestly started thinking I could pay the condo down really fast, like way faster than a normal person probably would with my income, and that I’d just figure it out somehow. And then I’d be thinking, okay, I can study for the CPA exams too, even though that’s a huge thing and I haven’t exactly had some perfect plan for it before. But during those charged-up times it was like, no, I can do that, I can pass them, I can move up, I can make more money.

And then I also got really convinced I could run a little bookkeeping business on the side, like after work, and somehow it would all fit together. Condo, extra payments, CPA studying, side clients — in my head it wasn’t even “maybe.” It was more like, of course I can do this, failure just isn’t an option.

Looking back, it feels kind of embarrassing because I know those are big things, and not impossible exactly, but the way I was thinking about them was… too certain. Like I suddenly had this belief that I was much more capable than usual and that if I just kept going, I’d make it all happen.

I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.\n\nBuying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you feel unusually energized or “up” compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.\n\nAnd at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think there was anything like… clearly dangerous, if that’s what you mean. Like I didn’t do something that immediately makes me think, “Oh, that was reckless.”

But I also keep second-guessing myself, because some things felt impulsive in the moment just because I was moving so fast mentally. Like I’d get an idea and feel this urge to act on it right away, instead of sitting with it or thinking it through the way I normally would. So I guess it depends on what counts as risky.

I don’t have one big example where I can say, “Yes, that was definitely risky.” It was more that I felt less cautious than usual, but I’m not sure I actually did anything that crossed a line.

I’m awake, and my thoughts keep going, and then I start noticing that I’m still awake, and that gets frustrating. But I don’t know if I’d label it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been feeling pretty down lately, more than I want to admit, probably. It’s like I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s already this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Not even like I’m reacting to a bad day—it’s just there.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be in my own condo, which I worked so hard to get, and I know I should feel proud or comfortable or at least okay there. But lately I’ll just sit there and look around and it makes me want to cry. Not because anything specific is wrong with the place, just because I feel so… empty, I guess. Heavy and empty at the same time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to explain, because it’s not like I made some big decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… stopped.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It was something I looked forward to, even if I was tired after work or didn’t feel like leaving the condo at first. Once I got there, the music and moving around and just being around people usually helped. But I haven’t been going. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.\n\nAnd painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.\n\nBuying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. And since then—probably about a month now—I’ve noticed this really obvious back-and-forth, where I had that more charged-up stretch and then this crash after it. The crash part especially has been hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the stressful thing was the mortgage becoming real. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you feel unusually energized or “up” compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.\n\nAnd at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.\n\nBut during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. I remember almost being surprised by it, because I’d look at the time and realize how little I’d slept, but I didn’t feel tired in the way I should have.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was resting well, exactly. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, were you doing more activities or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That was actually one of the things that made me realize something was off, because it wasn’t just that I had more energy in my head — I was doing a lot more.\n\nI’d get home from work, and normally after staring at numbers all day I want to eat something, maybe watch TV, maybe do one chore if I’m being responsible. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.\n\nAnd then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. Like I’d be writing down what I wanted to change, what it might cost, what order to do things in, looking up supplies, comparing ideas. Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates again, even though the mortgage was already done, like I needed to keep monitoring everything.\n\nAnd on top of that I started setting up ideas for side jobs, like ways I could bring in extra money. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that more charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to count it exactly, because it wasn’t like one clean block where I can say, “It started Monday and ended Friday.” The really obvious back-and-forth — like being all charged up and then crashing — has been going on for about a month now, basically since the mortgage became real and it wasn’t just an idea anymore.\n\nThe charged-up parts would come in stretches within that, but the whole pattern has been about a month. That’s when I started noticing, or maybe when it became harder to ignore, because everything felt more intense around then.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it at first as “talking more,” exactly, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining things that were important. But my sister definitely noticed.\n\nI remember one conversation where I was going on about the condo stuff, and it was like I jumped from equity to taxes to paint colors and then back to what I should do first, and she finally kind of laughed but also looked overwhelmed and said I was talking really fast. Like she couldn’t even get a word in.\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, once she pointed it out. It was like there was this pressure behind it, like if I didn’t keep going I’d lose the thread or the idea would disappear. I wasn’t trying to cut her off, but I kept pushing forward, almost like the words were coming faster than I could sort them.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did you feel more confident or capable than usual—to the point that your ideas or abilities seemed unusually strong or special?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. At the time it didn’t feel like “I’m being unrealistic” or anything. It felt like I could just handle everything if I pushed hard enough.\n\nI honestly started thinking I could pay the condo down really fast, like way faster than a normal person probably would with my income, and that I’d just figure it out somehow. And then I’d be thinking, okay, I can study for the CPA exams too, even though that’s a huge thing and I haven’t exactly had some perfect plan for it before. But during those charged-up times it was like, no, I can do that, I can pass them, I can move up, I can make more money.\n\nAnd then I also got really convinced I could run a little bookkeeping business on the side, like after work, and somehow it would all fit together. Condo, extra payments, CPA studying, side clients — in my head it wasn’t even “maybe.” It was more like, of course I can do this, failure just isn’t an option.\n\nLooking back, it feels kind of embarrassing because I know those are big things, and not impossible exactly, but the way I was thinking about them was… too certain. Like I suddenly had this belief that I was much more capable than usual and that if I just kept going, I’d make it all happen.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think there was anything like… clearly dangerous, if that’s what you mean. Like I didn’t do something that immediately makes me think, “Oh, that was reckless.”\n\nBut I also keep second-guessing myself, because some things felt impulsive in the moment just because I was moving so fast mentally. Like I’d get an idea and feel this urge to act on it right away, instead of sitting with it or thinking it through the way I normally would. So I guess it depends on what counts as risky.\n\nI don’t have one big example where I can say, “Yes, that was definitely risky.” It was more that I felt less cautious than usual, but I’m not sure I actually did anything that crossed a line.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, sometimes I’m thinking about things, like stuff I need to do or conversations from the day or whatever, but it doesn’t always feel like fear or panic. It’s more like my brain just won’t settle down, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as anxious. I’m not lying there thinking, “Oh no, something terrible is going to happen,” at least not usually. It’s more just... I’m awake, and my thoughts keep going, and then I start noticing that I’m still awake, and that gets frustrating. But I don’t know if I’d label it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been feeling pretty down lately, more than I want to admit, probably. It’s like I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s already this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Not even like I’m reacting to a bad day—it’s just there.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be in my own condo, which I worked so hard to get, and I know I should feel proud or comfortable or at least okay there. But lately I’ll just sit there and look around and it makes me want to cry. Not because anything specific is wrong with the place, just because I feel so… empty, I guess. Heavy and empty at the same time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to explain, because it’s not like I made some big decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… stopped.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It was something I looked forward to, even if I was tired after work or didn’t feel like leaving the condo at first. Once I got there, the music and moving around and just being around people usually helped. But I haven’t been going. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.\n\nAnd painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.\n\nBuying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. And since then—probably about a month now—I’ve noticed this really obvious back-and-forth, where I had that more charged-up stretch and then this crash after it. The crash part especially has been hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the stressful thing was the mortgage becoming real. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you feel unusually energized or “up” compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.\n\nAnd at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.\n\nBut during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. I remember almost being surprised by it, because I’d look at the time and realize how little I’d slept, but I didn’t feel tired in the way I should have.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was resting well, exactly. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, were you doing more activities or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That was actually one of the things that made me realize something was off, because it wasn’t just that I had more energy in my head — I was doing a lot more.\n\nI’d get home from work, and normally after staring at numbers all day I want to eat something, maybe watch TV, maybe do one chore if I’m being responsible. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.\n\nAnd then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. Like I’d be writing down what I wanted to change, what it might cost, what order to do things in, looking up supplies, comparing ideas. Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates again, even though the mortgage was already done, like I needed to keep monitoring everything.\n\nAnd on top of that I started setting up ideas for side jobs, like ways I could bring in extra money. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that more charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to count it exactly, because it wasn’t like one clean block where I can say, “It started Monday and ended Friday.” The really obvious back-and-forth — like being all charged up and then crashing — has been going on for about a month now, basically since the mortgage became real and it wasn’t just an idea anymore.\n\nThe charged-up parts would come in stretches within that, but the whole pattern has been about a month. That’s when I started noticing, or maybe when it became harder to ignore, because everything felt more intense around then.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it at first as “talking more,” exactly, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining things that were important. But my sister definitely noticed.\n\nI remember one conversation where I was going on about the condo stuff, and it was like I jumped from equity to taxes to paint colors and then back to what I should do first, and she finally kind of laughed but also looked overwhelmed and said I was talking really fast. Like she couldn’t even get a word in.\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, once she pointed it out. It was like there was this pressure behind it, like if I didn’t keep going I’d lose the thread or the idea would disappear. I wasn’t trying to cut her off, but I kept pushing forward, almost like the words were coming faster than I could sort them.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did you feel more confident or capable than usual—to the point that your ideas or abilities seemed unusually strong or special?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. At the time it didn’t feel like “I’m being unrealistic” or anything. It felt like I could just handle everything if I pushed hard enough.\n\nI honestly started thinking I could pay the condo down really fast, like way faster than a normal person probably would with my income, and that I’d just figure it out somehow. And then I’d be thinking, okay, I can study for the CPA exams too, even though that’s a huge thing and I haven’t exactly had some perfect plan for it before. But during those charged-up times it was like, no, I can do that, I can pass them, I can move up, I can make more money.\n\nAnd then I also got really convinced I could run a little bookkeeping business on the side, like after work, and somehow it would all fit together. Condo, extra payments, CPA studying, side clients — in my head it wasn’t even “maybe.” It was more like, of course I can do this, failure just isn’t an option.\n\nLooking back, it feels kind of embarrassing because I know those are big things, and not impossible exactly, but the way I was thinking about them was… too certain. Like I suddenly had this belief that I was much more capable than usual and that if I just kept going, I’d make it all happen.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think there was anything like… clearly dangerous, if that’s what you mean. Like I didn’t do something that immediately makes me think, “Oh, that was reckless.”\n\nBut I also keep second-guessing myself, because some things felt impulsive in the moment just because I was moving so fast mentally. Like I’d get an idea and feel this urge to act on it right away, instead of sitting with it or thinking it through the way I normally would. So I guess it depends on what counts as risky.\n\nI don’t have one big example where I can say, “Yes, that was definitely risky.” It was more that I felt less cautious than usual, but I’m not sure I actually did anything that crossed a line.\n\nDoctor: Did any one of those charged-up periods last for 4 days in a row or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s a little hard for me to pin down cleanly, because it hasn’t felt like one neat episode with a start and stop, if that makes sense. But the really obvious back-and-forth — like being in that charged-up stretch and then kind of crashing afterward — has been going on for about a month now.\n\nIt really started once the mortgage became real. Like, once it wasn’t just an abstract condo thing anymore, my brain sort of latched onto it, and since then I’ve noticed the cycling a lot more. So I can’t give you an exact day-by-day answer off the top of my head, but yes, there were definitely stretches in there that felt like they lasted several days, not just one afternoon or one night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… it has, I think. Not in a dramatic way where I stopped functioning completely, because I’m still showing up to work and doing my job, mostly. But it’s definitely started to show.

It’s not that they did anything wrong. I just don’t feel like myself, and I don’t want to have to explain that, or be around people and feel like they can tell something is off with me. So I guess it has caused problems, even if I’ve been trying to keep everything looking normal from the outside.

I’ll be exhausted, but not rested, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s just started to feel like it’s affecting everything, and I don’t really know how to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: When you’re lying awake, do you feel worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it worried, exactly. I’ve been feeling pretty down lately, more than I want to admit, probably. It’s like I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s already this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. But lately I’ll just sit there and look around and it makes me want to cry. Not because anything specific is wrong with the place, just because I feel so… empty, I guess. Heavy and empty at the same time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.\n\nAnd then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that more charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to count it exactly, because it wasn’t like one clean block where I can say, “It started Monday and ended Friday.” The really obvious back-and-forth — like being all charged up and then crashing — has been going on for about a month now, basically since the mortgage became real and it wasn’t just an idea anymore.\n\nThe charged-up parts would come in stretches within that, but the whole pattern has been about a month. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if anyone said it in those exact words, like, “You’re unusually driven,” but I think it was noticeable because I was doing way more than I normally would after work.

Like for a while I’d come home and instead of just making dinner or watching something and going to bed, I’d suddenly have this burst of energy and I’d start cleaning, then making these detailed renovation lists for the condo, then checking interest rates again, then thinking through side-job ideas and setting things up like I had to do it right then. It wasn’t just one productive evening. It was more like I couldn’t shut it off once I got going.

And I think people could tell because I’d talk about all these plans and jump from one thing to another, and it probably sounded like a lot. I remember feeling almost irritated if someone slowed me down or acted like maybe I should just relax, because to me it felt urgent and important. So… yeah, compared with my normal self, I was definitely more active and kind of driven.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.81, F32.9, F41.9, F43.9, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep. It’s gotten really, really messed up lately. Like, some nights I’m just up half the night, and it’s not even that I’m doing anything important—I’m just lying there with my mind running and running, like I can’t shut it off. And then other times I’ll wake up way too early, before I need to, and I feel like I barely slept at all. I’ll be exhausted, but not rested, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s just started to feel like it’s affecting everything, and I don’t really know how to get it back under control.\n\nDoctor: When you’re lying awake, do you feel worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it worried, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’m thinking about things, like stuff I need to do or conversations from the day or whatever, but it doesn’t always feel like fear or panic. It’s more like my brain just won’t settle down, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as anxious. I’m not lying there thinking, “Oh no, something terrible is going to happen,” at least not usually. It’s more just... I’m awake, and my thoughts keep going, and then I start noticing that I’m still awake, and that gets frustrating. But I don’t know if I’d label it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’ve been feeling pretty down lately, more than I want to admit, probably. It’s like I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s already this heavy, empty feeling sitting there. Not even like I’m reacting to a bad day—it’s just there.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I’ll be in my own condo, which I worked so hard to get, and I know I should feel proud or comfortable or at least okay there. But lately I’ll just sit there and look around and it makes me want to cry. Not because anything specific is wrong with the place, just because I feel so… empty, I guess. Heavy and empty at the same time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to explain, because it’s not like I made some big decision to stop doing things. It’s more like I just… stopped.\n\nI used to go to salsa class, and I really liked it. It was something I looked forward to, even if I was tired after work or didn’t feel like leaving the condo at first. Once I got there, the music and moving around and just being around people usually helped. But I haven’t been going. I keep telling myself, “Maybe next week,” or I’ll think about getting dressed for it, and then it feels like this huge amount of effort for something I don’t even feel excited about anymore.\n\nAnd painting too. I have my paints still sitting out on the table, like I was going to use them. They’re just there. I’ll look at them and it feels strange, like they belong to somebody else. Like I remember that I’m the kind of person who likes painting, but I don’t actually feel it right now. I don’t get that little pull to sit down and start. I just look at everything and feel nothing, which is kind of scary because those were things that used to feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t think I’m sitting there making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s more that when I’m really low, I get this kind of thought like, “What is the point of all this?” or I feel tired of having to keep doing everything, if that makes sense. But then I second-guess whether that counts as life not feeling worth living, because it’s not like I’m picturing a specific thing or wanting to do something to myself.\n\nIt’s more… I just feel really worn down sometimes, and I don’t like admitting that out loud because it sounds dramatic. But I haven’t been, like, planning to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, it kind of did. Not like one dramatic thing happened in a single day and then I woke up like this, but it seemed to get really obvious after the mortgage became real.\n\nBuying the condo was supposed to be a good thing, and I know it is, but once the papers were signed and it wasn’t just an idea anymore, it felt like this huge weight landed on me. Like, okay, now this is mine, this payment is mine, this responsibility is mine. And since then—probably about a month now—I’ve noticed this really obvious back-and-forth, where I had that more charged-up stretch and then this crash after it. The crash part especially has been hard to ignore.\n\nSo I guess the stressful thing was the mortgage becoming real. It’s been about a month of this being noticeable.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you feel unusually energized or “up” compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It’s hard to describe without making it sound like I was just in a good mood, because it wasn’t exactly that. It was more like I felt almost lit up, like everything in me was turned up higher than usual. I remember thinking, “Okay, I’ve got this, I can handle everything,” like I was suddenly unstoppable or something.\n\nAnd at first that felt kind of good, honestly, especially compared to now. Like I had more confidence than I normally do, and things felt more possible. But it wasn’t just happy or positive. I was also really quick to get irritated. My mom would ask me a normal question, something small, and I’d snap back like she was accusing me of something. Or my supervisor would ask about some little detail at work, and I’d feel this flash of anger, like, “Why are you on me about this?” even though normally I probably would’ve just answered.\n\nSo yes, I was more “up,” but also kind of edgy with it. Not calm energy. More like I was running hot.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, did you need less sleep than usual and still feel okay the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was one of the weirdest parts, honestly, because normally if I don’t sleep enough, I feel it right away. I’m dragging, I need coffee, I’m kind of useless in the morning.\n\nBut during that stretch, there were several nights where I only slept maybe three or four hours, and I still got up feeling… charged, I guess. Like I’d wake up and my body was already going, like I didn’t even need coffee. I remember almost being surprised by it, because I’d look at the time and realize how little I’d slept, but I didn’t feel tired in the way I should have.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was resting well, exactly. It was more like sleep just didn’t feel as necessary for a bit, which is not normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that more charged-up stretch, were you doing more activities or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That was actually one of the things that made me realize something was off, because it wasn’t just that I had more energy in my head — I was doing a lot more.\n\nI’d get home from work, and normally after staring at numbers all day I want to eat something, maybe watch TV, maybe do one chore if I’m being responsible. But during that stretch, I’d come home and suddenly start cleaning like the place had to be fixed immediately. I’d be wiping down cabinets, sorting things, moving stuff around, like I couldn’t just sit down.\n\nAnd then because of the condo, I started making all these renovation lists — not even realistic ones, necessarily. Like I’d be writing down what I wanted to change, what it might cost, what order to do things in, looking up supplies, comparing ideas. Then I’d switch over to checking interest rates again, even though the mortgage was already done, like I needed to keep monitoring everything.\n\nAnd on top of that I started setting up ideas for side jobs, like ways I could bring in extra money. I’d be making notes, looking at options, thinking maybe I could do bookkeeping on the side or organize something online. It was all after work, too. It wasn’t like I had free time. I just kept going like I couldn’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that more charged-up period last?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to count it exactly, because it wasn’t like one clean block where I can say, “It started Monday and ended Friday.” The really obvious back-and-forth — like being all charged up and then crashing — has been going on for about a month now, basically since the mortgage became real and it wasn’t just an idea anymore.\n\nThe charged-up parts would come in stretches within that, but the whole pattern has been about a month. That’s when I started noticing, or maybe when it became harder to ignore, because everything felt more intense around then.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it at first as “talking more,” exactly, because in my head it felt like I was just explaining things that were important. But my sister definitely noticed.\n\nI remember one conversation where I was going on about the condo stuff, and it was like I jumped from equity to taxes to paint colors and then back to what I should do first, and she finally kind of laughed but also looked overwhelmed and said I was talking really fast. Like she couldn’t even get a word in.\n\nAnd I could feel it, too, once she pointed it out. It was like there was this pressure behind it, like if I didn’t keep going I’d lose the thread or the idea would disappear. I wasn’t trying to cut her off, but I kept pushing forward, almost like the words were coming faster than I could sort them.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did you feel more confident or capable than usual—to the point that your ideas or abilities seemed unusually strong or special?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that’s a good way to put it. At the time it didn’t feel like “I’m being unrealistic” or anything. It felt like I could just handle everything if I pushed hard enough.\n\nI honestly started thinking I could pay the condo down really fast, like way faster than a normal person probably would with my income, and that I’d just figure it out somehow. And then I’d be thinking, okay, I can study for the CPA exams too, even though that’s a huge thing and I haven’t exactly had some perfect plan for it before. But during those charged-up times it was like, no, I can do that, I can pass them, I can move up, I can make more money.\n\nAnd then I also got really convinced I could run a little bookkeeping business on the side, like after work, and somehow it would all fit together. Condo, extra payments, CPA studying, side clients — in my head it wasn’t even “maybe.” It was more like, of course I can do this, failure just isn’t an option.\n\nLooking back, it feels kind of embarrassing because I know those are big things, and not impossible exactly, but the way I was thinking about them was… too certain. Like I suddenly had this belief that I was much more capable than usual and that if I just kept going, I’d make it all happen.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think there was anything like… clearly dangerous, if that’s what you mean. Like I didn’t do something that immediately makes me think, “Oh, that was reckless.”\n\nBut I also keep second-guessing myself, because some things felt impulsive in the moment just because I was moving so fast mentally. Like I’d get an idea and feel this urge to act on it right away, instead of sitting with it or thinking it through the way I normally would. So I guess it depends on what counts as risky.\n\nI don’t have one big example where I can say, “Yes, that was definitely risky.” It was more that I felt less cautious than usual, but I’m not sure I actually did anything that crossed a line.\n\nDoctor: Did any one of those charged-up periods last for 4 days in a row or longer?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s a little hard for me to pin down cleanly, because it hasn’t felt like one neat episode with a start and stop, if that makes sense. But the really obvious back-and-forth — like being in that charged-up stretch and then kind of crashing afterward — has been going on for about a month now.\n\nIt really started once the mortgage became real. Like, once it wasn’t just an abstract condo thing anymore, my brain sort of latched onto it, and since then I’ve noticed the cycling a lot more. So I can’t give you an exact day-by-day answer off the top of my head, but yes, there were definitely stretches in there that felt like they lasted several days, not just one afternoon or one night.\n\nDoctor: During one of those charged-up periods, did it cause problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. Not in a dramatic way where I stopped functioning completely, because I’m still showing up to work and doing my job, mostly. But it’s definitely started to show.\n\nAt work, I’ve made reconciliation mistakes that I really shouldn’t be making. Like, that’s literally part of what I do, so it’s not the kind of thing I can just brush off as, “Oh, everyone makes mistakes.” I mean, everyone does, obviously, but these were enough that my supervisor pulled me aside and asked what was going on. She wasn’t cruel about it, but it was embarrassing. I felt like I had to act like I was fine, but inside I was kind of panicking because I knew I wasn’t being as careful as I normally am.\n\nAnd socially… yeah, I’ve been avoiding people more. I usually go to salsa class, and I’ve skipped it, and I’ve been dodging friends or not really answering messages the way I normally would. It’s not that they did anything wrong. I just don’t feel like myself, and I don’t want to have to explain that, or be around people and feel like they can tell something is off with me. So I guess it has caused problems, even if I’ve been trying to keep everything looking normal from the outside.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up periods, did other people notice that you were unusually active or driven compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if anyone said it in those exact words, like, “You’re unusually driven,” but I think it was noticeable because I was doing way more than I normally would after work.\n\nLike for a while I’d come home and instead of just making dinner or watching something and going to bed, I’d suddenly have this burst of energy and I’d start cleaning, then making these detailed renovation lists for the condo, then checking interest rates again, then thinking through side-job ideas and setting things up like I had to do it right then. It wasn’t just one productive evening. It was more like I couldn’t shut it off once I got going.\n\nAnd I think people could tell because I’d talk about all these plans and jump from one thing to another, and it probably sounded like a lot. I remember feeling almost irritated if someone slowed me down or acted like maybe I should just relax, because to me it felt urgent and important. So… yeah, compared with my normal self, I was definitely more active and kind of driven.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
